<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YMCdkePo4hpmzDtePnMXVJ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C DIF rate extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$, 45--174$\times$ above baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected than procedural ones. The Simpson's paradox (\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains; ranking is preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but measurement properties drift across model generations.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.
13	
14	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
15	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
16	
17	We propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$); (2)~\emph{Flag} ($15\% \leq$ C\% $< 25\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\citep{clauser1998dif}. Temporal C\% complements static quality metrics~\citep{zhu2026bhi} as a ``temporal health'' dimension and identifies stable anchor items for equating frameworks~\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core.
18	
```

</details>

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch 补充] P28 修正 — 基于 Reviewer 独立评估的 3 项调整\n\n**P28 主指令已发，以下是修正项，覆盖原指令中对应部分：**\n\n---\n\n## 修正 1: SF-4 IRT Fit — 改放 Appendix（不是 Limitations）\n\n原 P28 要求在 limitations.tex 加 IRT fit 句子。**撤销该要求。** 改为：\n\n1. 在 `method.tex` §3.2 \"LLM-specific challenges\" paragraph 末尾加 1 句：\n   \"MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.\"\n\n2. 在 `appendix.tex` Statistical Considerations subsection 加 1 段：\n   \"\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). ```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C DIF rate extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$, 45--174$\times$ above baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected than procedural ones. The Simpson's paradox (\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains; ranking is preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but measurement properties drift across model generations.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.
13	
14	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
15	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
16	
17	\looseness=-1
18	We propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$); (2)~\emph{Flag} ($15\% \leq$ C\% $< 25\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\citep{clauser1998dif}. Temporal C\% complements static quality metrics~\citep{zhu2026bhi} as a ``temporal health'' dimension and identifies stable anchor items for equating frameworks~\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core.
19	
```

The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$); (2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15\\% \\leq$ C\\% $< 25\\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\\citep{clauser1998dif}. Temporal C\\% complements static quality metrics~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} as a ``temporal health'' dimension and identifies stable anchor items for equating frameworks~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core.\n",

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Methodological scope.}
4	MH-DIF detects only uniform DIF; logistic regression reveals an additional 24.8\% non-uniform DIF, making 31\% conservative. More fundamentally, DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently but cannot determine \emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts. A definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data. The semi-synthetic framework uses binary response flipping (incorrect $\to$ correct) to simulate differential contamination, representing a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern. Real-world contamination involves partial memorization, reasoning shortcuts, and format-specific advantages; detection power under such realistic patterns is untested. The moderate $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$ reflects sensitivity to severe differential behavior; the framework's primary methodological contribution---cross-subject matching achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$---is a matching strategy property independent of injection realism.
5	
6	\paragraph{Matching boundary and sample size.}
7	Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; when contamination spans ${>}10$ subjects (${>}18\%$ of MMLU), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069. The GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power and is exploratory; replication with larger model samples is warranted.
8	
9	\paragraph{Generalizability.}
10	All six benchmarks use multiple-choice or binary scoring; generalizability to open-ended generation (e.g., AlpacaEval), coding (e.g., HumanEval), or partial-credit benchmarks is untested and would require polytomous DIF methods. Temporal cohorts are defined by calendar year, a coarse grouping; finer-grained definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns. MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not require IRT model fit; for transparency, we note that the MMLU response matrix shows poor unidimensional 2PL fit (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark), which does not affect MH validity since stratification relies only on total score ranking.
11	
```

```text
14	
15	\subsection{Statistical Considerations}
16	\label{app:statistical}
17	
18	\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.
19	
20	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in \{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.
21	
22	\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.
23	
24	\section{Structural Analysis Details}
25	\label{app:structural}
26	
27	Three progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\% $= 38.1\%$, exceeding the 25\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.
28	
```

**5 lines before:**
```
The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.
```

**The LLM-specific challenges paragraph (last 10 lines):**
```
\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\% (\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\Cref{sec:structural}).
```

**5 lines after:**
```
\subsection{Cohort Designs}
\label{sec:cohorts}

We construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.
```

The paragraph is located at lines 34 in the file, spanning from the start of `\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.}` through the discussion of the four key challenges (local independence, model family independence, ability distribution non-normality, and multidimensionality).

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0188WeJzCjKf83EKrPsF1dyX`</summary>

```text
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.
33	
34	\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\% (\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\Cref{sec:structural}).
35	
36	\subsection{Cohort Designs}
37	\label{sec:cohorts}
38	
39	We construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.
```

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "03454b37-fb02-4f38-accb-629aac26d35b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "cbddeae6-604a-42c0-82fc-32a47b2f6d7a",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "587aceaa-c3a8-47fb-be11-87ea41092aac",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}).",
    "newString": "cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \\emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Zero FPR under single-subject cross-subject matching]\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",

          " ",
          " The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. ```json
{
  "type": "queue-operation",
  "operation": "enqueue",
  "timestamp": "2026-05-21T19:14:31.478Z",
  "sessionId": "00ba99eb-1333-4d2c-8a72-7c9ea72ef3de",
  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] Round 8 审稿修复 P29 — 3 MF + 10 SF + refs，DDL 2026-05-25。引用需要验证。\n\n编辑顺序：先做 MF2 术语替换（机械性、无风险），再做 MF1/MF3 discussion（叙事判断），最后 SF 微调。每完成一批做 mini compile check。\n\n---\n\n## Batch 1: 术语替换（MF2）+ 机械编辑（SF1, SF4）\n\n### MF2: Title + 全文 drift → instability\n- **Title** (main.tex): \"Temporal Measurement Drift in LLM Benchmarks\" → \"Temporal Measurement Instability in LLM Benchmarks\"\n- **全文替换**：搜索所有 .tex 文件中的 \"drift\"（约15-20处），按语境替换：\n  - \"measurement drift\" → \"measurement instability\"\n  - \"temporal drift\" → \"temporal instability\" 或 \"temporal DIF\"\n  - \"31% Measurement Drift\" (§4.2 title) → \"31% Measurement Instability\"\n  - \"items exhibit temporal drift\" → \"items exhibit temporal instability\"\n  - **保留**：如果 drift 出现在引用其他工作的语境中，不改\n  - **Table 2 caption**: \"None reduces baseline FPR below 31%\" → \"None reduces baseline C% below 31%\"\n\n### SF1: Proposition 1 → Remark\n- method.tex lines 50-53: 将 `\\begin{proposition}...\\end{proposition}` 改为 `\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}` 保留数学内容但去除 formal proof 格式。如果有 `\\newtheorem{proposition}` 定义且不再使用，可删除。\n\n### SF4: Table 2 列名修正\n- experiments.tex tab:structural_comparison (lines 56-72): \n  - 列名 \"Temp.\\ FPR\" → \"Temp.\\ C\\%\"\n  - Caption: \"None reduces baseline FPR below 31%\" → \"None reduces temporal C\\% below 31%\"\n  - Baseline 行的 \"FPR\" → \"C\\%\"\n\n---\n\n## Batch 2: 核心 Discussion 段落（MF1, MF3, SF3）\n\n### MF1: DIF Interpretation — 三层框架\n在 discussion.tex §5.1 \"Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability\" 中，替换当前内容为更精确的三层框架：\n\n核心论点（用这些素材重写，不是照抄）：\n- **Layer 1 (Impact)**：整体能力差异。已被 ability matching 消除（Cohen's d = -0.17, matched C% = 28.9%）。\n- **Layer 2 (DIF)**：控制能力后的残余 item-level 差异。这就是 31%/28-29%。92.4% item-specificity 表明这不是 subject-wide 效应。\n- **Layer 3 (Attribution)**：DIF 原因（contamination vs capability evolution vs training methodology）。超出本文范围，需 training data access。\n\n关键措辞：\n- \"DIF detects that items function differently, not why\" — 这是 diagnostic signal，不预设原因\n- 即使 DIF 反映 genuine capability change，measurement properties 仍然不是 invariant，score comparability 仍受影响\n- \"The practical import is identical regardless of attribution: temporal DIF means that the same benchmark score carries different meaning across model generations\"\n- Domain directionality (STEM favoring 2024 55.4%, Humanities favoring 2023 64.4%) 作为 descriptive evidence 而非 causal claim\n\n不要删除 §5.1 中已有的有用内容（Simpson's paradox 引用、ranking stability ρ≈0.997 等），而是重组。\n\n### MF3: 28-29% Convergence 讨论\n在 experiments.tex §4.4 Structural Analysis 末尾（或 discussion.tex §5.1 中）新增一段：\n\n核心论证（Reviewer 建议的精确措辞）：\n\"Random-split control (0.18%) confirms that the 28-29% convergence is temporal-specific, not a procedural artifact of MH-DIF. The convergence across multiple ability-matching methods (nearest-neighbor: 28.9%, Llama ability-matched: 28.3%, graduated de-duplication K=20: 29.1%) strengthens confidence that this reflects genuine item-level temporal variation, though causal attribution requires further investigation.\"\n\n补充论证：\n- Ability quintile comparison (2.9%) confirms that ability differences alone produce minimal DIF\n- The 28-29% floor represents the genuine temporal DIF after removing ability confounding (~2.4 pp reduction from 31.3%)\n- This convergence is a feature, not a bug — it shows robustness of the estimate\n\n### SF3: 强化 Practical Significance\n在 §4.6 Downstream Impact 或 §5.1 中，更 prominently 呈现已有的 family-level evidence：\n- Llama-3 derivatives: +53 median rank positions\n- Mistral: -35 positions\n- Cohort gap: 96.2 positions (40× > random removal, p < 0.001)\n- Discriminative signal: z = -8σ to -49σ\n- 结论：aggregate ρ≈0.997 masks family-level and pairwise-comparison shifts that matter for model selection decisions\n\n---\n\n## Batch 3: SF 微调 + References\n\n### SF2 (text only): ΔTPR 标注为 upper-bound\n- method.tex §3.4 injection framework: 在 ΔTPR 定义后加一句：\"Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported ΔTPR values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios.\"\n- experiments.tex §4.5: 同样标注 ΔTPR as upper-bound\n\n### SF5: Growing Pains 概念对比\n- related_work.tex: 扩展 Growing Pains 引用句，加入概念对比：\n  \"FPC methods assume a subset of anchor items with stable measurement properties; temporal DIF analysis directly tests this stability assumption and can identify candidate anchor items through DIF pre-screening, providing complementary input to equating procedures.\"\n\n### SF6: Cluster bootstrap → Future Work\n- §4.2 或 Limitations: 加一句 acknowledging family correlation in bootstrap：\"Model-level bootstrap may underestimate CI width due to within-family correlation; cluster bootstrap at the model-family level is a natural extension. The K=20 de-duplication analysis (C% = 29.1%) provides a conservative robustness check.\"\n\n### SF7: Logistic regression spec → Appendix\n- appendix.tex, 在 Statistical Considerations 后新增：\n  \"\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (Section 4.2) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (Section 4.4) uses logistic regression with a group × ability interaction term; a significant interaction (p < 0.05) indicates non-uniform DIF.\"\n\n### SF8: Power analysis parameter distribution (P2, if space)\n- §4.1 或 Appendix: 补充描述统计：\"2PL parameters from PSN-IRT show substantial heterogeneity: discrimination $a$ ranges from 0.2 to 3.8 (SD = 0.6), difficulty $b$ from -3.5 to 2.1 (SD = 1.1). The reported TPR of 0.68-0.80 reflects average-parameter items; low-discrimination items ($a < 0.8$) have reduced power.\"\n- **注意**：这些数字是 approximate — 如果你能从已有实验数据中找到准确数值（查看 artifacts 目录中任何 2PL 参数文件），使用准确数值。否则用 placeholder [TODO: verify] 标记，我会让 Agent 补充。\n\n### SF9: GSM1K → Appendix\n- experiments.tex §4.7: 将 GSM1K correlation 段落（\"An exploratory GSM1K analysis...\"）移至 appendix.tex。\n- §4.7 主文保留一句 summary：\"An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) suggests a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage (\\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}), though statistical power is limited and replication is warranted.\"\n\n### SF10: Permutation resolution note\n- experiments.tex §4.2: 将 \"p = 0.005\" 改为 \"p = 0.005 (resolution limit of 200 permutations)\"。补充一句：\"Complementary per-subject permutation analysis (1,000 permutations $\\times$ 8 subjects) yields mean null C\\% $= 0.09\\%$ with $p_{95} = 0.62\\%$, confirming the full-MMLU result.\"\n\n### Missing References\n在 references.bib 中添加（WebSearch 验证准确 bib 条目）：\n1. **Oren et al. (2024)** — \"Proving Test Set Contamination in Black Box Language Models\", ICML 2024\n2. **Deng et al. (2024)** — \"Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models\", NAACL 2024\n3. **Wang et al. (2024)** — \"MMLU-Pro: A More Robust and Challenging Multi-Task Language Understanding Benchmark\"\n4. **Gema et al. (2024)** — \"Are We Done with MMLU?\" (MMLU-Redux)\n\n引用位置：\n- Oren et al. + Deng et al.: §2 Related Work contamination detection 段落\n- MMLU-Pro + MMLU-Redux: §5.3 audit protocol 中 \"retire flagged items\" 建议旁\n\n### Q5: Audit threshold justification\n- discussion.tex §5.3: 在 graduated thresholds 后补充一句 justification：\"These thresholds are adapted from ETS practice, where C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$ triggers item review; the effect-size basis of ETS classification makes thresholds portable across examinee populations.\"\n\n### Q6: BHI speculative clarification\n- 当前措辞 \"could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices\" 已经是 speculative future direction，无需修改。\n\n### Holland & Thayer bib year check\n- 检查 references.bib 中 Holland & Thayer 条目年份是否正确（应为 1988）。Detailed comments 提到 OpenAlex 记录可能显示 1986 mismatch。\n\n---\n\n## 页面预算\n主文 8 页限制。MF1 Discussion 段落扩展 + MF3 convergence 段落约增加 0.5 页。SF9 GSM1K 移出主文约释放 0.3 页。净增 ~0.2 页。用 \\looseness=-1 和微调管理。Appendix 无限制。\n\n## Commit 策略\n每 batch 完成后 commit。最终 commit message 格式：`[paper] P29: Round 8 action matrix — terminology fix, DIF interpretation framework, convergence analysis`"
}

编辑顺序：先做 MF2 术语替换（机械性、无风险），再做 MF1/MF3 discussion（叙事判断），最后 SF 微调。每完成一批做 mini compile check。

## Batch 1: 术语替换（MF2）+ 机械编辑（SF1, SF4）

### MF2: Title + 全文 drift → instability
- **Title** (main.tex): "Temporal Measurement Drift in LLM Benchmarks" → "Temporal Measurement Instability in LLM Benchmarks"
- **全文替换**：搜索所有 .tex 文件中的 "drift"（约15-20处），按语境替换：
  - "measurement drift" → "measurement instability"
  - "temporal drift" → "temporal instability" 或 "temporal DIF"
  - "31% Measurement Drift" (§4.2 title) → "31% Measurement Instability"
  - "items exhibit temporal drift" → "items exhibit temporal instability"
  - **保留**：如果 drift 出现在引用其他工作的语境中，不改
  - **Table 2 caption**: "None reduces baseline FPR below 31%" → "None reduces baseline C% below 31%"

### SF1: Proposition 1 → Remark
- method.tex lines 50-53: 将 `\begin{proposition}...\end{proposition}` 改为 `\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}` 保留数学内容但去除 formal proof 格式。如果有 `\newtheorem{proposition}` 定义且不再使用，可删除。

### SF4: Table 2 列名修正
- experiments.tex tab:structural_comparison (lines 56-72): 
  - 列名 "Temp.\ FPR" → "Temp.\ C\%"
  - Caption: "None reduces baseline FPR below 31%" → "None reduces temporal C\% below 31%"
  - Baseline 行的 "FPR" → "C\%"

---

## Batch 2: 核心 Discussion 段落（MF1, MF3, SF3）

### MF1: DIF Interpretation — 三层框架
在 discussion.tex §5.1 "Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability" 中，替换当前内容为更精确的三层框架：

核心论点（用这些素材重写，不是照抄）：
- **Layer 1 (Impact)**：整体能力差异。已被 ability matching 消除（Cohen's d = -0.17, matched C% = 28.9%）。
- **Layer 2 (DIF)**：控制能力后的残余 item-level 差异。这就是 31%/28-29%。92.4% item-specificity 表明这不是 subject-wide 效应。
- **Layer 3 (Attribution)**：DIF 原因（contamination vs capability evolution vs training methodology）。超出本文范围，需 training data access。

关键措辞：
- "DIF detects that items function differently, not why" — 这是 diagnostic signal，不预设原因
- 即使 DIF 反映 genuine capability change，measurement properties 仍然不是 invariant，score comparability 仍受影响
- "The practical import is identical regardless of attribution: temporal DIF means that the same benchmark score carries different meaning across model generations"
- Domain directionality (STEM favoring 2024 55.4%, Humanities favoring 2023 64.4%) 作为 descriptive evidence 而非 causal claim

不要删除 §5.1 中已有的有用内容（Simpson's paradox 引用、ranking stability ρ≈0.997 等），而是重组。

### MF3: 28-29% Convergence 讨论
在 experiments.tex §4.4 Structural Analysis 末尾（或 discussion.tex §5.1 中）新增一段：

核心论证（Reviewer 建议的精确措辞）：
"Random-split control (0.18%) confirms that the 28-29% convergence is temporal-specific, not a procedural artifact of MH-DIF. The convergence across multiple ability-matching methods (nearest-neighbor: 28.9%, Llama ability-matched: 28.3%, graduated de-duplication K=20: 29.1%) strengthens confidence that this reflects genuine item-level temporal variation, though causal attribution requires further investigation."

补充论证：
- Ability quintile comparison (2.9%) confirms that ability differences alone produce minimal DIF
- The 28-29% floor represents the genuine temporal DIF after removing ability confounding (~2.4 pp reduction from 31.3%)
- This convergence is a feature, not a bug — it shows robustness of the estimate

### SF3: 强化 Practical Significance
在 §4.6 Downstream Impact 或 §5.1 中，更 prominently 呈现已有的 family-level evidence：
- Llama-3 derivatives: +53 median rank positions
- Mistral: -35 positions
- Cohort gap: 96.2 positions (40× > random removal, p < 0.001)
- Discriminative signal: z = -8σ to -49σ
- 结论：aggregate ρ≈0.997 masks family-level and pairwise-comparison shifts that matter for model selection decisions

---

## Batch 3: SF 微调 + References

### SF2 (text only): ΔTPR 标注为 upper-bound
- method.tex §3.4 injection framework: 在 ΔTPR 定义后加一句："Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported ΔTPR values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios."
- experiments.tex §4.5: 同样标注 ΔTPR as upper-bound

### SF5: Growing Pains 概念对比
- related_work.tex: 扩展 Growing Pains 引用句，加入概念对比：
  "FPC methods assume a subset of anchor items with stable measurement properties; temporal DIF analysis directly tests this stability assumption and can identify candidate anchor items through DIF pre-screening, providing complementary input to equating procedures."

### SF6: Cluster bootstrap → Future Work
- §4.2 或 Limitations: 加一句 acknowledging family correlation in bootstrap："Model-level bootstrap may underestimate CI width due to within-family correlation; cluster bootstrap at the model-family level is a natural extension. The K=20 de-duplication analysis (C% = 29.1%) provides a conservative robustness check."

### SF7: Logistic regression spec → Appendix
- appendix.tex, 在 Statistical Considerations 后新增：
  "\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (Section 4.2) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (Section 4.4) uses logistic regression with a group × ability interaction term; a significant interaction (p < 0.05) indicates non-uniform DIF."

### SF8: Power analysis parameter distribution (P2, if space)
- §4.1 或 Appendix: 补充描述统计："2PL parameters from PSN-IRT show substantial heterogeneity: discrimination $a$ ranges from 0.2 to 3.8 (SD = 0.6), difficulty $b$ from -3.5 to 2.1 (SD = 1.1). The reported TPR of 0.68-0.80 reflects average-parameter items; low-discrimination items ($a < 0.8$) have reduced power."
- **注意**：这些数字是 approximate — 如果你能从已有实验数据中找到准确数值（查看 artifacts 目录中任何 2PL 参数文件），使用准确数值。否则用 placeholder [TODO: verify] 标记，我会让 Agent 补充。

### SF9: GSM1K → Appendix
- experiments.tex §4.7: 将 GSM1K correlation 段落（"An exploratory GSM1K analysis..."）移至 appendix.tex。
- §4.7 主文保留一句 summary："An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) suggests a negative correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage (\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}), though statistical power is limited and replication is warranted."

### SF10: Permutation resolution note
- experiments.tex §4.2: 将 "p = 0.005" 改为 "p = 0.005 (resolution limit of 200 permutations)"。补充一句："Complementary per-subject permutation analysis (1,000 permutations $\times$ 8 subjects) yields mean null C\% $= 0.09\%$ with $p_{95} = 0.62\%$, confirming the full-MMLU result."

### Missing References
在 references.bib 中添加（WebSearch 验证准确 bib 条目）：
1. **Oren et al. (2024)** — "Proving Test Set Contamination in Black Box Language Models", ICML 2024
2. **Deng et al. (2024)** — "Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models", NAACL 2024
3. **Wang et al. (2024)** — "MMLU-Pro: A More Robust and Challenging Multi-Task Language Understanding Benchmark"
4. **Gema et al. (2024)** — "Are We Done with MMLU?" (MMLU-Redux)

引用位置：
- Oren et al. + Deng et al.: §2 Related Work contamination detection 段落
- MMLU-Pro + MMLU-Redux: §5.3 audit protocol 中 "retire flagged items" 建议旁

### Q5: Audit threshold justification
- discussion.tex §5.3: 在 graduated thresholds 后补充一句 justification："These thresholds are adapted from ETS practice, where C\% $\geq 25\%$ triggers item review; the effect-size basis of ETS classification makes thresholds portable across examinee populations."

### Q6: BHI speculative clarification
- 当前措辞 "could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices" 已经是 speculative future direction，无需修改。

### Holland & Thayer bib year check
- 检查 references.bib 中 Holland & Thayer 条目年份是否正确（应为 1988）。Detailed comments 提到 OpenAlex 记录可能显示 1986 mismatch。

### Tool result

_source line 6117 · 2026-05-21T19:14:55.963Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HbGiJG5jpp5QRcPQr9PLrs`</summary>

```text
0	\section{Introduction}
1	\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\% range 21--48\%, unweighted mean $\approx 30\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement drift does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\rho \approx 0.997$. The drift is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.
4	
5	A growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\%~\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}
10	\caption{Global vs.\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}
11	\label{fig:global_vs_differential}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	We apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.
15	
16	MMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\rho \approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).
17	
18	Our contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:
19	
20	\begin{enumerate}
21	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
22	
23	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
24	
25	\item \textbf{Temporal measurement drift at scale}: Approximately 31\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$ (45--174$\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).
26	\end{enumerate}
27

Crucially, this measurement drift does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. 79	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
80	\label{sec:downstream}
81	
82	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate ordering is preserved despite 31\% measurement drift. DIF-flagged items nonetheless concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), though a circularity ablation confirms DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive non-random strategy. Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming that DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal.
83	
84	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
85	\label{sec:gsm8k}
86	
87	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines. Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ versus $17.9$--$18.9\%$ for procedural ones, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to training data composition shifts.

```text
46	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.
47	
48	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested.
49	
50	\begin{proposition}[Zero FPR under single-subject cross-subject matching]
51	\label{prop:fpr}
52	Let $M_i^{(-s)} = \sum_{j \notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.
53	\end{proposition}
54	
55	When contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\leq$9\% of subjects ($k \leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
56	
57	Empirically, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.
58	
59	Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \Cref{app:method_details}.
60	
```

      "content": "\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Zero FPR under single-subject cross-subject matching]\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 46,
      "totalLines": 60
    }
  },

50	
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\%), making 31\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\%$ and temporal C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\%$---consistent matched values ($\sim$28--29\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items. Six alternative explanations (ability confounding, family inflation, $\theta$-conditioning, within-subject effects, ranking impact, local dependence) were tested; none reduces C\% below $\sim$28\% (\Cref{app:structural}).
55	
56	\begin{table}[t]
57	\centering
58	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces baseline FPR below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}
59	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
60	\small
61	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
62	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
63	\toprule
64	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ FPR & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
65	\midrule
66	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
67	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
68	$\theta$-cond. & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
69	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
70	\bottomrule
71	\end{tabular}}
72	\end{table}
73	
74	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
75	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
76	
77	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \leq 5$ ($\leq$9\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\%), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).
78	
79	\subsection{Downstream Impact}

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested---and existing contamination detectors validate against ground truth that conflates global exposure with differential functioning. We develop a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching that achieves zero false positive inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$), and show that web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$, $\phi = 0.012$)---exposing a category error in prevailing validation methodology. Applying MH-DIF with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 items), we find 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (ETS~C) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts, each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$). This does not threaten ranking validity ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but reveals which capability dimensions shift across generations. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\% of the signal is item-specific; DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$) with a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4

The Simpson's paradox (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains; ranking is preserved ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but measurement properties drift across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$); (2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15\\% \\leq$ C\\% $< 25\\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\\citep{clauser1998dif}. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core.
