    "newString": "this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement drift does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The drift is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).\n\nOur contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement drift at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).\n\nOur contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement drift at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).",

    "newString": "temporal measurement instability with validated",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement drift with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both.     "oldString": "\\begin{proposition}[Zero FPR under single-subject cross-subject matching]\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\\end{proposition}",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \\emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Zero FPR under single-subject cross-subject matching]\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C DIF rate extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$, 45--174$\times$ above baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected than procedural ones. The Simpson's paradox (\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains; ranking is preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but measurement properties shift across model generations.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.
13	
14	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
15	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
16	
17	\looseness=-1
18	We propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$); (2)~\emph{Flag} ($15\% \leq$ C\% $< 25\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\citep{clauser1998dif}. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core. Temporal C\% could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices~\citep{zhu2026bhi}.
19	
```

50	
51	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
52	\label{sec:structural}
53	
54	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\%), making 31\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\%$ and temporal C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\%$---consistent matched values ($\sim$28--29\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items. Six alternative explanations (ability confounding, family inflation, $\theta$-conditioning, within-subject effects, ranking impact, local dependence) were tested; none reduces C\% below $\sim$28\% (\Cref{app:structural}).
55	
56	\begin{table}[t]
57	\centering
58	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces temporal C\% below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}
59	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
60	\small
61	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
62	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
63	\toprule
64	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
65	\midrule
66	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
67	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
68	$\theta$-cond. & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
69	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
70	\bottomrule
71	\end{tabular}}
72	\end{table}
73	
74	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
75	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
76	
77	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \leq 5$ ($\leq$9\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\%), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).
78	
79	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
80	\label{sec:downstream}
81	
82	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate ordering is preserved despite 31\% measurement instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\sigma$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal), though a circularity ablation confirms DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive non-random strategy. Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming that DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal.
83	
84	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
```

     \subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
     \label{sec:disc_temporal}
     
     Temporal DIF analysis decomposes benchmark score differences into three layers. \emph{Layer~1} (impact): overall ability differences between cohorts, removed by matching (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; matched C\% $= 28.9\%$, reducing unmatched 31.3\% by $\sim$2.4~pp). \emph{Layer~2} (DIF): item-level differences persisting after ability control---the 28--29\% floor across multiple matching methods, of which 92.4\% is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). \emph{Layer~3} (attribution): whether DIF reflects contamination, capability evolution, or training methodology shifts---beyond observational DIF analysis without training data access. The practical import is identical regardless of attribution: temporal DIF means the same benchmark score carries different meaning across model generations.
     
     Even when DIF reflects genuine capability change, measurement properties are not invariant, and score comparability is affected. The Simpson's paradox (\Cref{sec:temporal}) confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than accuracy gains. Aggregate ranking is preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$), but family-level profiles reveal systematic shifts (Llama-3: ${+}53$ median rank; Mistral: $-35$) that matter for pairwise model selection. Domain directionality (STEM favoring 2024 at 55.4\%, Humanities favoring 2023 at 64.4\%) describes where measurement properties shift without implying a specific cause.
     ```

The practical import is identical regardless of attribution: temporal DIF means the same benchmark score carries different meaning across model generations.\n\nEven when DIF reflects genuine capability change, measurement properties are not invariant, and score comparability is affected. Aggregate ranking is preserved ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but family-level profiles reveal systematic shifts (Llama-3: ${+}53$ median rank; Mistral: $-35$) that matter for pairwise model selection. Domain directionality (STEM favoring 2024 at 55.4\\%, Humanities favoring 2023 at 64.4\\%) describes where measurement properties shift without implying a specific cause.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$) reflects that the two methods measure different constructs---global exposure versus differential functioning---making web-overlap labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$) confirms that MH-DIF detects differential behavior when present; the near-independence reflects a category error in the ground truth, not a detection failure.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose that benchmark maintainers run temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate, using cross-subject matching, with graduated action levels: (1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$); (2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15\\% \\leq$ C\\% $< 25\\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): rotate or retire flagged items. MMLU's C\\% exceeds the action threshold, aligning with ETS guidelines~\\citep{clauser1998dif}. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}; our open-source implementation runs in under 5 minutes on a single CPU core. Temporal C\\% could also serve as a dimension in composite quality indices~\\citep{zhu2026bhi}.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (details in \\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Measurement Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 95\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples). The rightward CI asymmetry reflects a heavy-tailed C\\% distribution across resamples, driven by subjects with C\\% exceeding 40\\%. Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%. Crucially, the temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ (below the $|d| = 0.2$ small-effect threshold) with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under strict 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching ($d \\approx -0.010$, $N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$---a 7.7\\% relative reduction from 31.3\\%, confirming that at most $\\sim$2.4 pp of observed DIF is attributable to ability mismatch. A permutation test (200 label shuffles) yields null C\\% $= 0.10\\% \\pm 0.12\\%$ ($p = 0.005$; 0/200 exceed observed), confirming that 31.3\\% cannot be attributed to chance.\n\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). Item-level regression shows 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). A Simpson's paradox confirms that DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate accuracy gains: domain accuracy positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$). The conclusion is robust across thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$; ETS~C's dual threshold makes results less sensitive to the number of strata $K$ than significance-only approaches ($K = 5$ per ETS practice~\\citep{donoghue1993mh}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. This near-independence extends to behavioral contamination detectors: the enrichment ratio of $0.964$ and Jaccard overlap of $0.206$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels imply that any detector validated against these labels---including ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}---is evaluated on a ground truth orthogonal to differential functioning.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$). Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\\%), making 31\\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3 C\\% from 61.3\\% to $28.3\\%$ and temporal C\\% from 31.3\\% to $28.9\\%$---consistent matched values ($\\sim$28--29\\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\\sim$28--29\\% of items. Six alternative explanations (ability confounding, family inflation, $\\theta$-conditioning, within-subject effects, ranking impact, local dependence) were tested; none reduces C\\% below $\\sim$28\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces temporal C\\% below 31\\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ C\\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond. & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \\leq 5$ ($\\leq$9\\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\\%), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate ordering is preserved despite 31\\% measurement instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8\\sigma$ to $-49\\sigma$ vs.\\ random removal), though a circularity ablation confirms DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive non-random strategy. Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming that DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines. Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (WinoGrande~\\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ versus $17.9$--$18.9\\%$ for procedural ones, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to training data composition shifts.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn GSM8K, semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). An exploratory GSM1K analysis ($N = 33$) shows a negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage, suggesting DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}).\n\nEven within a 6-month window (2023-H2 vs.\\ 2024-H1), DIF remains substantial (MMLU C\\% $= 34.35\\%$, TruthfulQA C\\% $= 31.33\\%$).\n",

        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \\emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ estimates correlate at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores in our data, confirming that computationally simpler CTT-based stratification introduces no bias relative to IRT ability matching.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$, 5,000 resamples), while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
        "numLines": 59,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 59
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches fail to reduce the temporal false positive rate below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal FPR unchanged at approximately 0.31. IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and FPR $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions in Appendix~Figure~A2).\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n",
